PEIPING. July 30 (Reuter). 
—The difficulties of the Japanese 
forces in Honan, who are fight- 
ing against large numbers of 
Chinese regulars and militia, 
are described by travellers pass- 
ing through here, 

In the recent drive from Honan~ 
Hupeh border’ to the north jn an 
attempt to entrap Chinese forces, 
the Japanese ore said to have used 
at least 250,000 with tanks ond acro- 


planes, but after months of fighting 
in which heavy casualties were 
suffered on both sides, little progress 
was made, The. major part of the 
province is still in Chinese hands.* 


Evory Man Armed 

The main reason for this is sald 
to be that-the Japanese were unable 
to put cnough men into the field to 
cope with the hordes of Chinese in 
Honan—practically every man en- 
rountered by the travellers was 
armed. Morale and discipline are 
high although ‘armament is inferior 
to the Japanese. — 

The travellers describe terribic 
scenes: of devastation in every, clty— 
the Japanese have been systematic 
ally bombing all clties in Chinese 
territory, the majority of which are 
virtually levelled to the ground, But 
thls, according. to the travellers, Is 
rerving to strengthen the morale ‘of 
the Chinese force and populace. .

portation of munittons js mule eart, 
upon which China, it, appeors, Js now. 
velying. -

DESIGNS ON 
- TURKEY: 


‘Nazis Adopt A New 
Technique - 


ISTANBUL, July 30 (Reuter). 
—German efforts to influence 
Turkey have taken a new line 
rainee the brusque—rejection—of 
foreign interference by Dr. 
Refik Saydam, the Turkish 
Prime Minister, in his speech to 
the Assembly on July 12, and the 
subsequent departure of Herr 
the German 
Ambassador, for Berlin. 

Bait For Businessmen 

Now instead of threats to poll- 
ticluns, Nazi agents here are concen- 
trating on Turkish businessmen, 
endeavouring to win them over to the 
doctrine that Turkey's real interests 
lie in the development of trade with 
Germany, and_using the signature ot 
the recent Turko-German Trade 
Treaty as the basis. 


Circulars discussing this argument] 9. 


have been recently distributed and a 
number of leading articles have 
appeared in the Turkish Press. Some 
of these articles express a fear lest 
such developments .might lead to 
German trade domination in Turkey.

New York Comment

“New York Times” says: to-day that 


the ‘effect of the French defeat was! 


fo lend everybody to expect, that the 
Blitzkrieg method would at onte be 
turned on England,

age, considering what they hod seen 
across the Channel, the British have 
prepared for the Invasion of thelr 
Inland, 

There will be no surprise or panic 
$n any English hamlet if the Germans 
land to-morrow, . 

But there Is also the possibility that 
Hitler nay shrink until the end from 
exposing on expeditionnry force to 
certaln, decimation. : ay 

It would not, be strange If the War 
Lord who dictated armistice terms to 
France ot the scene of the German 
surrender in 1918, were tempted by 
the same sense of drama and revenge 
to do hls utmost, to conquer Britain 
by starving her out, If he yields to 
this temptation. of: attempting 4 
blockade before an invasion it will be 

. good news fot the British, for thls 
kind of war they chose In, the first 
place, since it enables them to use to 
thy limit the strongest weapon they

HAVANA, July 30. (Reuter). 
—The main points of the Con- 
vention and the “Act of Havana” 
concluded by the Pan-American 
Conference are .riow published 
here and are as follows: ; 

_ "Considering. the destinies of the 
colonies ,of non-American countries 
fn this hemisphere, and that a‘situa- 
tlon, may develop which may .cx- 
tInguiah or materinily impulr their 
sovereignty, the American Republics 
condemn all vidlence. ; 

“The Republics consider possible 
transfers of sovereignty os against 
American sentiments and, the rights 
of American states to maintain their 
security and political Independence, 


. Transfor Unacceptable 

“No such’ transfer would be re- 
cognised or accepted by the American 
Republics no matter what form was 
employed. 

“They reserve the right to judge 
whether any transfer or attempted 
transfer has -the effect of smpairing 
the political independence of 
European possessions in thls hemls- 
phere, > . : 

“The Republics will establish 4 
provisional regime for such regions 
until such time og thelr definitive 
government Is established by free de- 
termination of the people. 

Unquestioned Right 

The Convention continucs: 

“Republics hifve the unquestioned 
right to tale such reglons under their 
administration and deliberate as 10 
thelr destinies, though this does not 
imply abrogation of principle of non- 
intervention. In the cose where u 
provisional regime is decided on, ad- 
ministration will be exerelsed by onc 
or more of the American States. 

“The Admintstration shall be in the 
interests of security of the Americas, 
and for the benefit of the region ad- 
ministered,” ‘ 

The Convention enumerates the in- 
dividual rights which are to be pre- 
acrved by the Administration and the 
States, : 

“Open economic relations shall bo 
malntained with all countries on the 
basis of reciprocity, The first Ad- 
ministration shall be for three years, 
ren¢wable for periods not more than 
ten yéars, i 

“Local revenues will finance the 
Adminstration. . 

Administrative Commission 

“A Commission for. territorial ad- 
ministration {s to be established and 
composed of one representative ‘from 
each country ratifying the Conven- 
tion, Any signatory. may .convoke 
the Commission and two thirds of the 
members constitute a quorum neces- 
sary to take decisions. : 
+ “None of the provisions of the 
Convention refer to territories which 
are ‘subject of dispute or claims be- 
tween European powers und one or 
more of the Republics of the 
Americas. 

“The Convention shall enter into 
force when two-thirds of the Re- 
publics have ratified it. : 

“The Act of Havana repeats the 
principles of the Convention and au- 
thorises the immediate creation of an 
emergency committee composed of 
one representative of each of the 
American republics, two-thirds of the 


membership_belng—.suMcient_for_a. 


Committee to act.” 

Argentina adds the reservation that 
it. regards the Falklands og part of

TALK :ON | 
AUSTRALIA 


Rotary Club Speaker 
Extols Attractions 


A talk on Australia was given at 
yesterdoy’s mecting of the Hongkong 
Rotary’ Club by Rotarian A. O. 
Smith.’ Dr, Arthur Woo, President 
of the Club, introduced the speaker 
ng one of the early members of the 
Hongkong. Rotary Club, who had re- 
tired to Australia and then returned 
to this Colony. 

Mr, Smith sald he had had many 
requests for information on Aus- 
tralln, and he hoped to prove that Jt 
was the plact for elther o holfday or 
retirement. He spoke of the history 
of the country. since 1788, when 
Captain Phillip sailed Into Botany 
Bay with 1,000 men. Australia was 
25 times bigger than the British 
Isles, and had 9 population of nearly 
7,000,000, af whom 97 per cent were 
of Britlsh stock, and 77,000 were 
aborigines. After’ describing the 
wealth, cultivation ond Industry of 
the country, the speaker said that the 
ear ond the acroplane.had done much 
to open up the fsolated fastnesses and 
to improve the conditions under 
which settlers lived. 

To the principle.of “White Aus- 
tralfa,” .all political parties In the 
Commonwealth subscribed because 
the white man's standard of ving 
would be endangered if, coloured 
labour were introduced. 

Some of the points that made Aus- 
tralin attractive to live In were the 
cheaper pound and the good Interest 
rates on investnents. The. climate 
was very genial, and snow and sun 
sports could be Indulged Jn; some 
sport nll the year round, In the 
south one occasionally found it cold 
enough to justify an overcoat, é 

The Customs regulations were 
exceedingly’ generous to’ white 
settlers, admitting free, furniture and. 
houschold goods to the value of £100 
for each ‘adult, two children counting 
as one adult for this purpose. The 
speaker said he would not recome 
‘mend taking In heavy furniture but 
a. Tlentsln carpet, blackwood stools 
and teapoys and camphorwood chests 
would bo sullable. The rooms were 
on the whale, smaller than onc found 
jn the Far East. Travellers from 
oversens could get a double allow- 
ance of luggofe free on railways If 
they: took the precaution of getting a 
chit from the purser of the ship be- 
fore landing. 5 

Cost Of Living 

The cost of living was low In Aus- 
‘tralia, despite the high standard of 
Hving, The Arbitration Bonrd of 
the Commonwealth Court last year 
assessed the maintenance\of a work- 
ing man, wife and two children at 
£4.1.0. a week in New South Wales 
and .£3,16.0 os the lowest in any 
State. ~ ae : 

The actual cost largely ‘depended 
on individual tastes. Boarding house 
rates were £1.5.0 to’ £1,15,0; hotels 
on the room and breakfast plan, from 
6s. a.day to 20s. a day; unfurnished 
flats could be had for £1,100 to £2 
a week, and up to £4 0 week for 
furnished tlats. A’ £700 five-roomed 
cottnge In a. suburb of Adctalde 
would be Ict unfurnished for £1,3.0. 
a week, : 

Servants were rather a luxury in 
Australia, and were not acctistomed 
to working Jale, Hotel and Club 
bars all closed at 6 p.m, 

Rotarian C. M. Manners thanked 
the speaker. It was 40 years since 
che had_visited Australin, ho said. but

‘NEW YORK, June 12.—The importance of a boxing 
championship to the holder thereof was strikingly revealed 


by George Parnassus, 
Ceferino Garcia had 


the garrulous little Greek, after his 
blown the world middleweight title 


‘to Ken Overlin in their recent bout here, ; ca 

When Overlin’s hand was lifted at the end. of the 15 
rounds it wiped out about $50,000. in purses which had 
been lincd up for the Filipino former ‘champion and Par- 


nossus. coed 


As a champion, Garcin was a 


drawing card in every spot that 
could boast of some local pride 
and joy who fancied himsclf a 
good: middleweight. , 

As an ex-champion, Garcia is Just- 
another fighter. ; 

His loss of the title doesn't mean 
that his income has stopped entirely, 
and, among other things, he will get 
a pretty good payday on September 
12 in a rematch With Overlin here at 
Madison Square Garden, But he 
won't be making as much money In 
the future as he did between Octo- 
bor, when he knocked out Fred Apos- 
toll for the champlonship; and May 
23, when he blew it to Overlin.

t

uch_os_.Henry Armstrong,_welter=

Ing powers,

ingly even in over-the-welght

WHEN IT WAS THOUGHT that things were 
nicely settling down, and at a period when one 
was about to set out confident that for the first 
time in many weoks one ‘could set foot on firm 
dry ground, came the typhoon and deluge turning 
Colony courses once again into lakes and swamps. 
“"" Te washed away what ‘local “copy” there might have 
been, but, fortunately, .a few. further details of the U.S. 
National Open and the play-off have come to hand. 


In a way, perhaps, the com- 
petition proper, when Lawson 
Little, Gene Sarazen and Ed. 
Oliver (subsequently disquali- 
fied) tied, was more thrilling 
than the ‘play-off: The play-off 
may have .earned for: Little a 
place among the greatest golfers 
to-day, but Sarazen’s ‘indomit- 
able courage and thrilling finish- 
ing burst in the competition 
gave him the right of way into 
Golf’s Hall of Fame. 


The penalty inturred by Ed. Oliver 
—dsqualifcation for starting nhead 
of schedule—seetns drastic, but_ it 
was also shared by five others, But 
they find not the mortification of 
having had frst place snatched from 
thelr grasp, 


He teed off thirty minutes be- 
fore his scheduled time, and falled 
to notify the official starter. is 
Drilllant last round of : 71—one 
under par—gave him 287, but It 
was posted on the board. 


here was ua strong movement to 
force the U.S. Golf Association to 
admit‘him into the play-off, but the 
Association were adamant over the 
breaking of one of more sacred rules. 

Little and Sarazen were most will- 
ing that Oliver should be included, 

Saracen: “Shucks. Let him play. 
I can veat both of them.” 


° . i) 
OWEVER, Sarazen’s clec- 
trifying finish provided the 

second sensation of the tourna- 
ment, : 


Little, home first with 287, 
sat in the Press tent and 
watched the board. Sarazen 
needed an almost impossible 34 
to tie, and the course was muddy 
and heavy from rain. 


He did it, and, not only that, 
nearly won the title outright on 
the last green when he just missed 
with a 45-foot putt It was a 
finish that was os sensational as 
that of 1932 when with a record 
breaking round of G6 he snatched 

the title” fron Bobby ~ Crultshank 
and FP. Perkins, - : 


Over those’ last nine holes, he 
needed two birdies to draw’ level 
with Little, and he got them at the 
lith and 13th, “From then on he 
necded par figures to keep level with 
the Jeader. He did it though the 
16th, but the 17th nearly lost it for 
aim. : 


From the rough on the right he 
pitched 30 fect post the pin, but 
without a moment’s hesitation he 
smacked his ball, firmly. ond it 
rattled in the cup to maintain the 
average. The 18th par was coms 
paratively: simple. : 


° + . 


EANWHILE, Little watched with 

anguished eyes, wondering 

what, if Sarazen could-play like that, 
would the morrow bring. 

But as things turned out he need 
never: have worrled. He went out 
in -34 and came home in 36 for a 
two-under-par 70, while Sarazen was 
out in ‘27 and home in 36 for 73. -’ 

Little won the first two holes, and 
from then on kept ahead of Sarazen 
from tee to green.

‘locker room, veteran ..Gene 
stated that this would probably 
be his last. season of competitive

fought Davo Crowley of England, 

Steve sports a terrific punch with 
elther fist, and ‘greatly impressed the 
‘boxing writers when, in a prelimin-

he knocked .out Vic~ Dellicurt! in 
quick time and with a fine display 
of ‘boxing.

they started again. We've been very 
busy. When a convoy comes in 
everybody comes on duty, whatever’ 
hour of the day or night, and we 
work until they are all fixed up and 
in-bed. Then the staff on duty. carry 
on and the rest’of us go back to bed. 
Night duty js diMcult here because. 
the black out is not 100 per cent., so 
we have to fo groping about the 
wards with a small hand torch. 


. The boys’ are -really marvellous. 
Never complain. Always cheerful. 
Always say: “Well st might have 
been worse.” We don't mind how 
hard we work or what we have to 
put up with. We've had a few Ger- 
mans to look after and they were 
good patients too,” © : i 


The most touching thing I have 
seen was one of our Tommies, A 
batch was getting ready for em- 
burkatlon to England. All our boys 
had had 4 parcel from home or a 
little present from someone. <A 
razor, shaving soap, clgarettes, some- 
thing, There was one German, ond 
of course he had no present. . So one 
of cur boys qui¢tly slipped out and 
bought him something. I'm pretty 
hardened, But it brought tcars to 
my eyes. I couldn't help it. 

June 17%, Well at last I am on a 
British ship heading for Old Eng- 
land. What oa four days we've had! 
Bombing, bombing all the time. - It's 
amazing how you yet used to it. Got 
sepnrated from F. for the first time 
since we came over. She was on 
day. duty, I was on night. At 4,30 
she was put on a train for St 
Nazaire. This is our last evacuation 
1 keep on thinking ubout F. und 
wondering {f she {s all right. 

I left with the last lot at 7,30 p.m. 
It's only about 10 miles to St. 
Nazaire, the port where we were to 
get ao ship, but It was'10 o'clock when 
we arrived, About 150 of us sisters 
were put on a tender with 500 troops. 
In the darkness the tender crept 
about trying to find a ship.. Sudden- 
ly a destroyer loomed ahead. The 
officer shouted that he had 500 men: 
could they take them on board? . The 
answer came “Yes.” Then he called: 
“And I've got. 150 sisters.” A blank 
silence followed that announcement, 
Then after a few moments: “You 
had better follow me. We've got no 
accommodation for women.” ‘ 

So the tender followed the de- 
stroyer for a while until we came 


‘olofigslde’-a7~Mner—and-we-were~ all 


taken aboard. Still we weren't out 
of the wood. They were bombing 
every ship they could find, but we 
didn’t get hit. It tool us two days 
to cross. And were we glad to sec, 
England! . 
June 19. First thing I did when 
I got to London was to go to head- 
quarters. I found out there that F, 
was safe and I was never so gind to 
see anyone in my Ilfe. She told me 
that two bombs had dropped one at 
each end of the ship they were on, 
while it was walting In the harbour, 
but nobody got a scratch. The 
‘planes pursued them, too, after they 
were at sea but nobody was hurt. 
She’s lucky to be alive. ‘Her chief

Bill,_13—has_bank-roll. 


Bill Murray, thirteen-year-old schoolboy evacuated from 
Dagenham, Essex, to rural Saltford in Somerset, might find 


‘plenty of amusement in his out- 


fields about his new home; but he’s not a chap 


grow under’ his feet. 

Bill's mother, a widow, lives In| 
Hedinghaim-road, Dagenhum. He 
knows what oo strugute she has to 
make ends meet. So instead of 
wasting his thue bird-nesting he fs 
doing a Nttle bread-winning. 

Bill got the sdea when ‘his clothes 
became shabby. He didn’t like to 
write to his mother for more, so he 
looked around for a spare-time job. 
Eventually he got a job delivering 
milk. 

“On his first pay-day he showed 
his wages, five shillings, fo his 
foster mother, Mrs, L. Darney, and 
she persuaded him to open a bank- 
ing account at the post office. Into 
this BIN put four shillings a week. 


The bad wenther came, snow 
eighteen inches deep in_ the lanes 
But that didn’t deter Bill: Every 
morning, before it was light, he was 
up and out on his round, 

Soon he had enough in his account 
to buy na complete rig-out—a sult, 
boots, pullover two caps, stockings, 
tie and underclothing. - His account 
mounted ngain. Cash and parcels of 
yrocery went to -his -mother. 

-Additional. jobs In gardens have 
augmented -his bank roll. 


Now August ts looming large on 

+ BIWs horizon. For then he will be 
leaving school and‘returning home 
to a.full-tlme job, with -his bank 


pook in a pocket of another new. 


“sult. | 
And this time . . . greatest joy of 
pa. a. he will have long trousers.

_ LONDON, July 30 (Reuter). 
~The first Army Victoria 
*. Crosses in the present war are 
awarded to, Captain Harold 
"Marcus Ervine-Andrews, of the 
East Lancashire Regiment, and 
the Inte Lance-Corporal Harry 
Nicholls of the Grenadier 
Guards. 

Captain Ervine-Andrews’ award is 
-for most conspicuous gallantry on 
the night of May 31—June 1. He 
took over about 1,000 yards of the 
defences In front of Dunkirk. For, 
over ten hours he and his company 
hetd. thelr position in the face of an 

Intense “attack by vastly superlor 
forces. ‘ 


5 
Auditor 


Brilliant Exploit 


Reinforcements were unable to 
reach him when the enemy attacked ' 
on both flanks, Capt, Ervinc- 
Andrews headed a group of volun- 
teers when one of his platoons was 

‘ fn danger and then going forward, 
climbed on top of a straw-roofed barn 
where he personally accounted for 17 
of the enemy with a rific, and many 
more with a Bren gun... 

After all ammunition was expend- 
ed, Capt, Ervine-Andrews collected 
_the remalning eight men of the com~ 
puny from thelr forward position ond 
when almost completely surrounded, 
led them back to cover, swimming or 
wading up to the chin in water for 
over a mile. ee

the company safely back, he again 

, took up his position, 0

haste The Phase Of

BUCHAREST, .July 30 (Reu- 
ter).—The situation in the Bal- 
kans appears to have passed the 
phase of negotiations. ~ 

The Italion correspondent of the 
“Bukerester Tageblatt’ says that the 
Axis powers “have confined them- 
selves to giving friendly odvice and 
ure vigilantly watching to see that no 
outside Power interferes, in south- 
east Europe for the purpose of main- 
tulning the existing quarrels.” ' 


Italian Paper Confiscated 


An interesting sidelight the 
situation was confiscation in 
Bucharest yesterday of the Italian 
newspaper, “Corriere della Sera.” 
The puper contained an article udvis- 
ing Rumania to reach an under- 
standing with the Bulgarians, who it 
is said, were demanding, territory in 
Dobrudja as far as Mangilia and even 
Constanza, . 

“It added that Russia was support- 
ing Bulgaria in this- claim becuuse 
Rumonian policy was ‘pro-German 
and’ the Soviet wished to eliminate 
Italian and German influence In the 
Balkans. 

Rumanian Reactions 


Rumanian — Press generally 
hails with. satisfaction the results of 
the Salzburg and Rome talks os out- 
lined in the Rumanian Government) 
communique, and emphusises the] 
importance of the role preserved for 
Rumania in the re-organisation of 
south-east, Europe under Axis super~ 
vision. : ‘

Instead, it is directed at all 
nations, including neutrals, and 
therefore can and should be 


resisted by all nations.

guccesses. Swarms - of tanks,

DIARY OF A BRITISH NU 

This” is the diary of a New 
Zealand Army Nursing Sister who 
went .to France immediately after 
war was declared and was one of the 


She tells the war 
of herself 


last to leave. 
experiences 
Australian friend 


sto tell them at home when I get baci 
to New Zealand. Have mnde friends 
with a girl from Queensland. Found 
out we came over In the same boat 
and occupied the same cabin but that 
I came a trip later. 

Sept. 22. Have had a week of do- 
ing nothing, but to-day started in on 
the job. We're a marvellously happy 
unlt and matron is wonderful. We're 
going to make up for Inst week In 
the next few days, The roulette 
tables and other heavy furniture have 
been removed from the Casino but 
we've got to do all the rest, There 
are to be 7 wards with 180 beds. 
We've scrubbed and cleaned to-day 
until we're all fired out, It’s still 
nl chaos but we'll. have it In some 
sort of working order !n no time. 
When it's full, we shall be busy. . 

Sept, 30. The diary has had to go 
—too much to do, Some partitions 
have been put up and a lot of paint- 
ing done. It's beginning to get the 
real hospital look. The blacking out 
was difficult, but it’s finished now. 
We've inspected the cellar which !s 
marked down us our refuge in case 
of alr raids. So far no ‘trouble of 
that, kind. There's no hot water 
laid On and no gas, We have to boil 
it all on primus ol] stoves. An emer- 
gency operating theatre has been got 
ready. : 


Oct, 9, We've settled down now 
to a routine. No wounded yet, but 
some sick cases. The engincers have 
fixed up hot water in the “theatre”, 
but there's still none in the wards, 
They're going to build us o new 
operating theatre. Supplies for it 
will be brought from London, and 
they say they are to be first class, 
We have marvellous billets. Moved 
some time ago into one of the hotels 
on the “front.” 

Everything Js done for our come 
fort. Our day {s—up at 6.30, break- 
fast7. When we go across to the 
hospital we have to wear our tin hats 
and have the gas masks at the alert: 
It's about o quarter-of-a-mile walk. 
We're a very happy unit. Some of 
us lunch at 12, come on agaln and 
work till 5, . 

The others Junch at 1, are off duty 
until 5, and then work until 8 when 
the night staff comes on. Last weck 
we got a gas ring In cach ward, but 
there is still no hot water, Rows 
and rows of beds now, where a few 
months ago pcopie were playing 
roulette. The theatre where audi- 
ences used to sit and Iisten to enter- 
_tainments.is.now_full_of beds. Get-

ready daylight when the ‘all’ clear”

DESIGNS ON 
~ TURKEY - 
Nazis Adopt A New 
Technique ; 


—German efforts to influence 
Turkey have taken a new line 
since the brusque rejection of 
foreign interference by, Dr. 
Refik Saydam, the Turkish 
Prime Minister, in his speech to 
the Agsembly on July 12 and the 
subsequent departure of Herr 
Franz von Papen, the German 
Ambassador, for Berlin. 

; Bait For Businessmen 

Now instend of thrents to poli- 
liclans, Nazi ngents here are concen- 
(ruting on Turkish businessmen, 
endeavouring to win them over to the 
doctrine that Turkey's real interests 
lle In the development of trade with 
Germany, and_using the signature of 
the recent Turko-German Trade 
Treaty. as the basis, - . 

Circulars discussing this argument 
have been recently distributed and a 
number of Icading articles have 
uppeared in the Turkish Press, Some 
of these artlcles express a Year Jest 
such developments might lead to 
German trade domination in Turkey.

ISTANBUL, July 30 (Reuter). | 


| ‘Wednesday, 
Emergency 


Courts 


Lords Gives New Bill 
Second Reading 


LONDON, July 30 (Reuter). 
—In the House of Lords to-day, 
Viseount Simon movod_ the 
second reading of the: Emer- 
gency Powers Bill to enable 
Government to establish, special 
courts in an emergency. 

Viscount Simon said there was 

general desire to avold soldiers hav- 
Ing to try ond punish civilians, 
Therefore we should: have a civil: 
court,’ 
- He udded that people must not be 
condemned on’vague suspicion but 
properly tried and he | strongly 
{favoured provistons for review in 
serious cases, 

Lord Mottistone (Liberal) move a 
rejection of the Bill. : 


Labour's Attitude 


Lord Nathan (Labour) said the 
Labour Party weré prepared to sup- 
port the Government in any measure 
to promote the prosecuilon of the 
war, but this BI) would not advance 
or retard the prosecution of the war, 

Lord Crewe (Liberal) suld that bn 
ylew of the marked differences be- 
tween the present meastire and the 
form in‘which {it was orlginally ine 
troduced, he would not oppose it, 

The Lord of Appeal expressed 
anxiety at_ the unlimited powers 
given the Government without any 
express sufeuards, but thought that 
the House should give the Bill’ a 
second reading. 

Viscount Cecit said he did not share 
the anxiety with regard the possi- 
bility of diminution of the rights of 
a subject under the Bill. He thought 
it was really a Bill for safeguarding 
the rights of a subject. 


Government's: Reply 


Viscount Simon, replying, referred 
to the question of the position of 
members of -the Home Guard which 
hod been raised by sume of the 
gpeakers. He contended that they 
had oll the respponsibilities and were 
entitled to all the privileges of a 
soldier. 

Dealing with the position of, the 
ordinary civilian who took up arms, 
Lord Simon snid If he was a france 
tireur it did not follow that he wus 
not a very brave and public- 
spirited man. 

Lord Simon said the new courts 
would apply the criminal law of the 
country and admitted that it wos an 
experiment, He described the Bill as 
one for the protection of — eivillan 
rights in times of a great crisis. He 
added, replying to a point raised .by 
Lord Strabolg!, that the privilege’ of 
peers with regard to the trials of 
certain offences was quite unaffected 
by the proposals, : : 

The Bill was read a second time 
without division,

LONDON, July 80 (Reuter). 
~"'Reuter’s”. special correspon-

frontier telegraphs that | the 
tragedy of a great colonial army 
torn between loyalty to its own 
Government and to Its old ally 
ig being enacted in Syria. 

Rumours of disturbances, including 
allegations of Arab nationalist 
risings {n Aleppo, are ‘devoid ‘of 
truth, The country is at present 
absolutely quict though the situation 
is ane of considerable confusion. 

A number of Frenchmen, chiefly 
oMcers, have crossed the frontier and 
Jolned. the Britlsh forces, but large 
numbers remain carrying on ‘their 
duties normally, though — locally« 
engaged soldiers from Egypt and the 
ndjacent territories hnve been demo- 
billsed and stnt home. 

Financial Problem 

One of the major problems is that 
the precarious financial situntton in 
France {s being reflected in’ Syria 
where the Jocal currency js bused on 
the franc, and this fs affecting the 
entire economy, giving rise to the 
poasibllity of inflation. —~ : ‘ 

Meanwhile: a rigid censorship is 
causing confusion and suspicion 
umung the Syrians: and French 
soldiers. : 

All foreign newspapers are banned,

Eugene Mittelhauser had crossed into

ter's” correspondent learns that he 
was recitled to Vichy. After Genernl 
Mittelhauser's departure, his Aide. 
de-Camp resigned and flew'to Egypt 
fo enlist with the British. : 

Soldiers are beginning to recelve 
mall and to learn for the first time

opinjon agninst the Petain Govern-

Disaffoction Forcast 

LONDON, July 30 (Router). — | 
General de Gaulle, speaking in the 
B.B.C.'s French service to-night, said 
that one of the first results of the 
armistice terms accepted by the 
Petain Government would be dis- 
affection. and probably ' revolt. of 
natives of the French Empire. . 

He declared; “These people, true 
to France, confident in France and 
respectful to France, view with In- 
dignation this capitulation of the 
Empire without fighting.” 

General de Gaulle concluded: 
"Since it Is obvioua that/the men who 
are saving their skins at Vichy are 
the subservient tools of the enemy's 
wishes, I affirm ‘Jn the name of 
France that the Empire must not 
submit to their disastrous orders.”

and cargoes would be Hable: to 
seizure by British patrols.

granted on the scate,of Imports ade-. 
quate for newtrals’ domestic consump- 
tion, 

His Majesty's Government had 
decided that. they must treat all 
metropolitan France as well as 
Algeria, Tunisia and French Morocco 
in the sume degree, for purposes of 
contraband and enemy export control, 
‘as enemy ‘control territory. : 

Benofits Neutrat.Trado 

Mr. Dalton said these meusures 
‘would greatly benefit honest neutral 
trade, At the sume time a heavy 
blow would be struck at those who 


sought to elude the British control or 


to carry goods cither .to or from 
cnemy territory, 

He declared that the Germain armics. 
had succeeded In over-running sarge 
parts of Western Europe, but oversens. 
imports which they required were 
still barred from scan commanded by 
the Reyal Navy. 

But to apply control at sen in the 
old way would mean diverting many 
ships far out of thelr course to con- 
trabund bases In British ‘waters elther 
in this island or in West Africa. It 
had been, suggested in some quarters 
that they Intended to extend the 
blockade to certain neutral countries.

neslst neutral countries to obtain

either fist, and greatly impressed the

of boxing.

crenm of the west’s college track- 
men including Mr. Jeffrey himself,

the young Salinas” specdster, is 
destined for cinder immortality.

cord, Then he stepped out in the

rousing time of 9.5, cracking the 
junior college record, coming with~

nearest competitor by 10 yards.

in nnother 9.5, but what of it?) That's 
great running and it's’ just what

ford senior. .

to-day.

WAR FACTORS IN |The Havana| 


FAVOUR OF BRITAIN 


LONDON, July 30 (Reuter).—The military correspondent of 
the “Yorkshire Post” to-day summarises the factors in Britain’s 
favour at the present juncture.of the war. 


He says that firstly the German 
army failed, in June to follow up the 
withdrawal of the B.E.F.,, when there 
was a chance. : 7 

Secondly, we have now a mighty 

- force in Britain and strong defences. 

Thirdly, the RAF, has achieved 
moral superiority. 

Blockado Success 

Fourthly, the blockade is closing 
the last loopholes of supply. 

Fifthly, the enemy power has falled 
tu Interrupt tmperial and American 
sources of supply, 

Sixthiy, there is more food and 
more materials in England than cver 
before, and the Navy ‘sweeps the 


BOB, 

Seventhly, Hitter’s forces ure wide- 
ly dispersed on a sea line from the 
Aretic to the Pyrenees, 

Eighthly, bis vast coastline has not 
enough ships to protect ft or to escort 
maritime invasion. ‘ 

Italy A Burden 

Ninely,. Italy's position is a burden 
‘on Germany, who may have to give 
material ald, especially If Maly a8 at- 
tacked as our main offensive, 

The correspondent also points out 
that) Britain controls the Mediter- 
ranean and has separated Italy from 
her ‘colonies: He adds that we are 
confident of repelling mussed ale 
atticks ond our’ fighter planes In- 
crease dally. . 

Furthermore, we hive achieved 
superiority in bombing «atticks, 

We are united ind resolute while 
Hitler has trouble In his conquered 
Jands as well as with the Balkans. 


‘by starving ber out. 


Finally, Russia again. cramps Ger- 

tnun freedom of action,

Convention 
Detailed Analysis Of 


Main Points 


HAVANA, July 30 (Reuter). 
—The main points of the Con- 
vention and the “Act of Havana” 
concluded by the Pan-American 


Conference are now published | 


here and are as follows: 

“Considering the destinies of the 
colonies of non-American countries 
In this hemisphere, and that a situn- 
ilon may develop which may ex- 
tinguish or materially impulr their 
sovereignty, the American Republics 
condemn all violence. ‘ 

“The Republics consider possible 
transfers of sovereignty as against 
American sentiments and the rights 
of Americnn states to maintain thelr 
security and political independence. 

Transfer Unacceptablo 

“No such transfer would be re-, 
cognised or accepted by the American 
Republics no matter what form was 
employed. 

“They reserve the right to judge 
whether any transfer or attempted 
transfer hus the effect of impairing 
the politica! independence of 
Europeun possessions in this hemis- 
phere, 

“The Republics will establish a 
provisional regime for such regions 
unt ‘such time as their definitive 
government is established by free de- 
termination of the people. 

Unquestioned Right 

The Convention continues: 

“Republics hifve the unquestioned 
right to tuke such regions under their 
administration and deliberate as to 
thelr destinies, though this does not 
imply abrogation of principle of non- 
intervention, In the case where a 
provisional regime'is decided on, nd- 
ministration will be exercised by one 
or more of the American States. 

“The Administration shall be In the 
interests of security of the Americas, 
und for the benefit of the region ad- 
ministered," , 

The Convention enumerates the in- 
dividual rights which are to be pre- 
served by the Administration und the 
States, 

“Open economic relations shall be 
muintained with all countries on the 
busis of reciprocity. The first Ad- 
ministration shall be for three years, 
renewable for periods not more than 

ars : 


Adminstration. 
Administrative Commission 

“A Commission for territorial ad- 
ministration is to be established and 
composed of one representative from 
each country rutifylng the Conven- 
tion. Any signatory may convoke 
the Commission and two thirds of the 


members constitute a quorum neces- 
sary to take decisions. 

“None of the provisions of the 
Convention refer to territories which 
ore subject of dispute or clulins be- 
tween European powers and one or 
more of the Republics of the 
Anericas. . 

“The Convention shall enter Into 
force when two-thirds of the Re- 
publics have ratified it. 

“The Act of Havana repeats the 
principles of the Convention and au- 
thorises the immediate creation of an 
emergency committee composed of 
one representative of cach of the 
American republics, two-thirds of the 
membership being sufficient for -a 
Committee to act.” 

Argentina adds the reservation that 
It regards. the Falklands as ‘part of 


-| Argentine territory.

Conscription 
Postponed 


Action By U.S. Sonate 
Committce — 


WASHINGTON, July 30 (Reuter). 
—The Senate Military Affairs Com- 
mittee to-day postponed final action 
on the Burke-Wadsworth Compulsory 
Milltary Service Bill, the aim’ of 
which was to register 42,000,000 men 
between the ages of 18 and 64, 

The .Committee instead decided to 
take up oa measure authorising Pre- 
sident Roosevelt to call up .the 
Natlonal Guard, ° : 

Tentative Approval 

The Sennte Committce have al- 
ready given tentative approval to the, 
main features of the Conscription 
Bijl, but there are signs of frowing 
opposition to the measure both inside 
and outside Congress. : 

One possibility, which the Com- 
mittee decided, is a reduction of the 
nge limits of those subject to active 
military training, to 21 to 31 instead 
of the present figure of 21 to 45, 


~ DIARY OF A. 
BRITISH NURSE.

they starfed again.’ We've been very 
busy. When n convoy comes in 
everybody comes on duty, whatever 
hour of the day or night, and we 
work until they are all fixed up and 
inf bed. Then the staff on duty carry 
on and the rest of us go back to bed, 
Night duty is diMcult here because 
the black out Is not 100 per cent., so 
we have to go groping: about the 
wards with a small hand torch. 

The boys are really marvellous, 
Never complain. Always cheerful. 
Always say: “Well ft’ might have 
been worse. We don't mind how 
hard we w "k or what we have to 
put up with = We've had a few Ger- 
mans to look after und they were 
good patients too, 

The ‘most touching thing 1 have 
seen was one of, our Tommies. A 
batch was getting ready for em- 
barkation to England. All our boys 
had had a parcel from home or a 
littie present from someone, A 
razor, shaving soap, cigarettes, some- 
thing. There was one German, and 
of course he had no present. So one 
of our boys quietly sllpped out and 


-bought — him—something.—I'm_ pretty. }- 


hardened. But {t brought tears to 
my eyes. I couldn't help it. 

June 17. Well at last I am on ao 
Britlsh ship heading for Old Eng- 
land. What a four days we've hud! 
Bombing, bombing all the time. It's 
amazing how you get used to it. Got 
separated [rom F. for the first tlne 
since we came over. She was on 
day duty, I was on night. At 430 
she was: put on/a_ troin for St. 
Nazaire. This ig our last evacuation 
nbout F. and 
wondering if she is ‘oll right, 

I left with the last lot at'7.30 p.m. 
It's only about 10 mil to St, 
Nazaire, the port where we were to 
xet a ship, but it was 10 o'clock when 
we arrived, About 150 of us sisters 
were put on a tender with 600 troops. 
In the darkness the tender erept 
about trying to find a ship. Sudden- 
ly a destroyer loomed ahead. The 
officer shouted that he hnd 600 men: 
could they take them on board? The 
answer came “Yes.” | Then he called: 
“And I’ve got 150 sisters,” A blank 
silence followed. that announcement. 
Then, after a few moments: “You 
hod better. follow me. We've got no 
accommodation for women.” 

So, the: tender’ followed the de- 
stroyer for a-iwhile until we came 
alongside. a Uner and we were all 
taken aboard. “Still we werén't out 


of the wood. - They were bombing |- 


every ship they could find, but we 
didn't get hit, ‘It took. us two days 
to cross. .And were we glad to sce 
England! Laas 

_ June 19. First. thing I. did when 
I got:to London was to go to head- 
quarters.. I found out’ there ‘that F. 
was safe and I was never'so glad to 
see anyone, in my ilfe, She told.me 
that two bombs had dropped one at 
each end of the ship thoy were on, 
while It was waiting in the harbour, 
but nobody,got -a scratch. , The. 
‘planes ‘pursued them, too, ufter they 
were ot sea‘ but nobody was hurt. 
She's lucky to be alive...Her chlef 
worry is whether she “will ever ‘see 
her ‘trunk again ‘as.she hoes only the 
clotlies she stands up In.

British Victory Means 


Much To. Arabs 


HEARTS of Italy's op- 
pressed Arab subjects in 
Libya are thrilling with a 
foretaste of deliverance. 


The swift) mouth-to-mouth 
telegraphy of the desert is 


spreading among them the news | 


that British armoured patrols, 
operating against the Italian 
forces ‘have passed beyond the 
sacred centre of Javabrib, 

Jarabub possibly does not menn 
much to Englishmen, On the map’ 
itt is Just a thy dot 20 miles inside 
the Libyan frontier. 

Frog 4 military point of view, it 
is the only point in the Italians’ 
original outpost Ine still cheld by 
them until you reach the outworks 
of the coastal stronghold of Bardia, 
100 miles farther north, + : 


But to Libyan Arabs, Jarabub 
is the Jocal Westminster Abbey, 
> the hollest shrine of their tribes. 
For among the sparse palm trees 
und scattered vegetable patches of 
Its Hitle oasis, their great) religious 
leader, Sayed Mohammed Ibn Ali 
Es Senussl, founded 100 years ago, 
the flerce Moslem puritan) move- 
ment ivhich swept North Africa, ° 
In its wWhile-domed mosque, be- 
nenth an exquisitelyswrought brass: 
wrille, rests the body of the Grand 
Senussi himself. 
The Itallans put down the Senussi 
movement with an iron hand, kill- 


ing the leaders or driving them into 
exile, 

This campatin of persecution was 
completed nearly ten years ago: and 
Jarabub became on more than a 
garrison post. 

But to the Arabs Its sentimental 
importance Is great. 


Though the British raiding 
column has not occupied Jarabub, 
having merely driven through it 
along the desert truck of Masrab 
EL Gebel leading to the North- 
West, the blow to Itallan prestige 
and the uplifting of religious hope 
nre both bound to be great. 

Our armoured columns have not 
confined their operations to this 
southern raid, the first yet reported 
on this front. , 

They have also. been raiding alone 
the coast towards Bardia: and Sidi 
Azelz, to the south of it, -— 

Both these places are jnsite’ the 
most, highly fortified zone in Htallan 
Libya. 

Our troops ure now asking. when 
they will have the-pleasure, of meet- 
ings ren) Italian troops in battle. 

So far they. have met only the 
conscripted Libyan Arab levies, who 
so hate their oppressors that. they 
not only desert after mobilisation, 
but even defy the Italiuns and re- 
fuse to put on uniform. 

Calvo had its second alr-raid alarm 
this morning, but no planes were 
sighted. 

, Three of the victims of Alex- 
andria’s Saturday raid have died, 
bringing the death-roll up to five,

with hard labour at Liverpool,

Corporation-road, Birkenhead, plead- 
ed guilty to giving informntion at 
Lourenco Marques, Portuguese East 
Africa. : 

It was alleyed {hat after he had 
been introduced to the German by. 
American sailors, he gave away the 
naine of his ship and when it was 
leaving port. Then he gave correct 
details of the ship’s armaments and 
her, port of destination. He men- 
tloned that his ship had been at- 
focked by U-boats, and gave details 
of damage to the convoy, :

you In Hamburg." -

spoke to the German as well, and I

German,

“SOLDIERS 
RECEIVE 
THE V.C: 


First Awards Of The 
’ Present War. © 


LONDON, July 30 (Reuter). 
—The first Army Victoria 
Crosses in the present war are 


awarded to Capliiin Harold’ 
Marcus Ervine-Andrews, of the 
East Lancashire Regiment, and 
the late Lance-Corpora): Harry 
Nicholls of the Grenadier 
Guards. 


Captain Ervine-Andrews saward Js 
for most conspicuous gallantry .on 
the night of May 31—June 1. He 
took over about 1,000 yards of the 
defences in front of Dunkirk. For 
over ten hours he and his company 
held thelr position in the face of an 

_ Intense attack by vastly superior 
forces. : 


Brilliant Exploit 


Reinforcements were unable to 
reach him when the cnemy attacked 
on both flanks, Capt... Ervine- 
Andrews headed a group of volun- 
teers when one of his platoons was 
In danger ond then going forward, 
climbed on top of a straw-roofed barn 
where he personally accounted for 17 
of the enemy with a rifle and many 
more with a Bren gun. 


After. all ammunition was expend. 
ed, Capt. Ervine-Andrews collected 
the remalning eight men of the com- 
puny from their forward position and 
when almost completely surrounded, 
Jed them back to cover, swimming or 
wading up to the chin in water for, 
over a mile. HV nt 


Having brought the remainder of 
the company safely back, he again 
took up his position. 

“Throughout this action, - Capt. 

Ervine-Andrews displayed a courage, 
tenacity and devotion to duty worthy 
of the highest traditions of the Bri- 
tish Army." . 
. Captain Ervine-Andrews js 29 
years of uge and kerved on the North- 
‘West Frontier of India in 1936-37 and 
wag mentioned in despatches. 


Carried On Though Wounded 


Lance Corposal Nicholls continued 
to lend his section of the platoon al- 
though wounded in the arm by shrop- 
nel, The company was subjected to. 


heavy machinegun fire as Jt came, 


over o small ridge, : 
_ Nicholls,selzed a Bron: gun, dashed 


towards ‘the ‘enemy machine-guns, |. 


firing from the hip, and silenced three 

* machine-guns olihough: again gevere- 

Jy wounded. ‘He then engaged Ger- 

man Infantry, causing many casualt- 

> fes ‘and firing until his ‘ammunition 

| was expended. -He was wounded aot

Due Here

The American Clipper, which for 
six months has been on the trans- 
Atlantic air service, this morming 
hopped off from San Francisco on its 
first trip across the Pacifle, with 
Hongkong as its destination. 


This has been revealed: by the local 
oMces of Pan-American Airways. 

The Americun. Clipper is a sister 
ship to the Callfornia and Honelulu 
Cllppers, and is, well known.on the 
Atlantic, having carried the frst con- 
signment of red cross materials from 
the United States to Europe during 
the present war. .

Attacks With R.A.F. 


LONDON, July 30 (Reuter).—An Air. Ministry communique 
states that our bombers yesterday made daylight raids over a 
widespread area in Germany and the Low Countries. 


A large supply ship was damaged 
near Flushing, barges ‘and other 
vessels were hit at Emden and Ham- 
burg nnd off the fsland of Terschell- 
ng. 

An oll refinery in the Rubr was 
bombed and attacks were made on 
several acrodromes in Germany und 
Hojtand. 

One of our bombers was Jost, 

Bad Weather Conditions 

During last night, although condi- 
tlons of visibility were poor, a large 
number of our bombers penetrated 
into enemy territory. Twenty-four 
alreraft, however, wre unnble to 
locate the targets assigned to them 
tind returned without dropping their 
bombs, : 

The remninder attacked targets in 
north-west Germany, the Ruhr and 
the Low Countries, including oll re- 
finecries, shipping, docks, acrodromes 
and roud and rail communications in 
these operations, 

Enemy's Hoavy Losses 

Full reports of yesterday's encoun- 

ters around our coasts show that a 


telal of 21 


enemy alreraft were 
destroyed, ° 


Two of our. fighter pilots are miss-, 


ing. 

To-day. although enemy activity 
has been restricted, two enemy 
bombers ond one enemy fighter so 
far have been destroyed,

HONGKONG’ TELEGRAPH 


DIARY OF A BRITISH NURSE 


Sept. 11, Got“my orders this 
morning to report at C—on Wednas- 

ay. 
ready and to-morrow shall say good- 
bye to all my friends. We- are 
allowed to fake a-trank, a sultcnse 
and a handbng, Lucky I had finished 
my lost private case. How flad | 
nm that I came to England last March 
and that I stayed ‘on. Now that it 
has come I would have joined up at 
home, anyway, But being here saves 
so much time. ag 

Sept, 13. Reported at C—this 
morning and was sent on to A—. 
Its the thirteenth, I wonder if it 
will be lucky for me. Everybody is 
very kind and everything is done for 
our comfort. I like the Jook of the 
other sisters in our unit, They ore 
of mixed ages—trom twenties up to 
57—80 of us in the unit. Some have

British Contraband 
Control! ‘Policy 
LONDON, July 30 (Reuter). 
—Continuing his statements in 
the House of Commons to-day, 
Mr. Hugh Dalton, Minister of 
Economic Warfare, stated that 
in the absence of navicerts, ships 
and cargoes would be liable to 
seizure by British patrols. 


He added thht navicerts would-be 
granted on the scale of imports ade- 


quate for neutrals’ domestic consump- 


tion. 

His Majesty's Government had 
decided that they must tfcat all 
metropolitan France as well as 
Algeria, Tunisia and French Morocco 
in the sume degree, for purposes of 
contraband and encmy export control, 
as enemy control territory. 

Bencfits Neutral Trade 

Mr, Dalton said. these measures 
would greatly benefit honest neutral 
trade. At the same time a heavy 
blow would be struck at those who 
sought to elude the British control or 
to carry goods elther to ‘or from 
enemy territory. 

He declared that the German armies 
lind succeeded ‘In over-running large 
parts of Western Europe, but oversens 
imports which they required. were 
atill barred. from seus commanded by 
the Royal Navy. ~ i 

But to apply control at sea in the 
old way would mean diverting many 
shlps far aut of their course to con- 
trabund bases In British waters either 
in thig island or in West Africa; It 
had been suggested In some quarters 
that they Intended to extend the 
blockade to cerjuin neutrat countries. 
That: was not so. : ; 

In addition to granting navicerts, 
the Government would follow a policy 
not merely to allow adequate supplics 
for domestic consumption to pass 
through British controls, but to 
asain neutral countrics to obtain 

hem, 


DESIGNS ON 
_ TURKEY. 


Nazis Adopt A New 
Technique 


ISTANBUL, July 30 (Reuter). 
—German efforts to influence 
Turkey have taken a new line 
since the brusque rejection of 
foreign interference by Dr. 
_ the Turkish 
Prime Minister, in his speech to 
the Assembly on July 12 and the 
subsequent departure of Herr 
Franz von Papen, the German 
Ambassador, for Berlin. 

Bait For Businessmen 

Now instead of threats to poli- 
ticlans, Nazi agents here are concen- 
trating on Turkish - businessmen, 
endeavouring to,win them over to the 
doctrine that Turkey's reol interests 
lie in the development of trade with 
Germany, and using the signature af 
the recent Turko-German Trade 
Trenty-na-the-basis.

Holland and Belgium and

the Pope yesterday, presented

mentation on the status of 
Catholics in the occupicd areas 
of Holland. .

Luigi Arrigoni, Counsellor Apostolic 
Nuncintor at Brussels, who was re- 
ceived in private audience by the 
Pontift Jast Saturday,’ presented a 
similar documentation regarding 
Belgian Catholics. 

Monsignor Clemente Micara, 


Apostolic Nunclo to Belgium present- 
ed the first data in this connection on 


July 20. s . 
Somo Difficultics 

Vatican sources revealed. that these 
negotiations are at present procecd- 
ing between the Reich authorities and 
the Holy See and ‘ore encountering

that certain prominent Dutch and

ant role in the intervention of their 
countries in the confilct.-

In this connection the reports cme 
phasize that during tHe past war, both

approved Mons, Mitara’s work in 
Belgium where he was Auditor of 
the- Brussels Nunciature.

‘CONFUSION 
“IN SYRIA. 


. French Colonial Army 
- . Bewildered 


LONDON, July 30 (Reuter). 
—Reuter's” special correspon- 
dent on the Palestine-Syriv 
frontier telegraphs that © the 
tragedy of a great cclonial army 
torn between loyalty to its own 
Government and to Sts old ally 
is being enacted in Syria. 

Rumours of disturbances, including 
allegations of Arab nationalist, 
are devold “ot” 
truth, The country {s at present 
nbsolutely quiet though the situntion 
is one of considerable confusion. 

A number of Frenchmen, chiefly 
officers, have crossed the frontler and 
joined the British forces, but large 
numbers’ remain carrying on their 
dutics normally, ‘though — locally- 
engaged soldiers from Egypt and the 
adjacent territories have been demo- 
bilised nnd sent home. ~ 

Financial Problem 

One of the major problems is that 
the precarious finunciat situation in 
France js being reflected in Syria 
where the local currency Is based on 
the franc, and this {s affecting the 
entire economy, giving rise to ‘the 
possibillty of inflatlon. 

Meanwhile a rigid censorship is 
causing confusion and: suspicion 
among the Syrians and French 
soldiers. . 

All foreign newspapers are banned. 

Contrary to reports that General’ 
Eugene Mittelhauser had crossed into 
Palestine to join the British, “Reu- 
ter’s” correspondent learns that he 
was recalled to Vichy. -After General 
Mittclliauser's departure,, his Aide- 
de-Cump resigned and flew to Egypt 
to enlist with. the British. 

Soldiers nre beginning to reccive 
mail and to learn for the first time 
of the hardships their families are 
enduring at German hands, This is 
causing many to become still more 
bitter, and there js a hardening of 
opinion against the Petain Govern- 
ment, : 

Disaffection Forecast 

LONDON, July 30 (Reuter).— 
Genernl de Gaulle, speaking in the 
B.B.C.'s French service to-night, sald 
that one of the first results of the 
armistice {terms accepted by the 
Petain Government would be dis- 
affection and probably ‘revolt of 
natives of the French Empire. 

He declared: “These people, true 
to France, confident in France -and ‘ 
respectful to France, view with in- 
dignation this capitulation of the 
Empire without fighting.” 

General .de Gaulle concluded: 
“Since it is obvious that the men who 
are saving their skins at Vichy are 
the subservient tools of the enemy's 
wishes, I offirm in the name of 
France that the Empire must not 
submit to their disastrous orders.”

53 HOMES RAZED 
IN BIG FIRE | 


BPECIAL TO TPE "TELEGRAPH" 

CAMDEN, New Jersey, July 30, 
(UP).—A fire In a paint factory kill- 
ed five and Injured 200 workers here 
to-day, 

The damage Is estimated at $2,000,- 
000. A thousand people have been 
rendered homeless, the fire destroy- 
ing 53 homes before it was controll- 
ed. 

A state of emergency was declared 
and 75 families in -nearby houscs 
were ordered to move. The New 
Jersey. National Guard was summon- 
ed to maintain order. 

The first of a series of 20 to 25 
explosions occurred in. a small room 
ina building where 160 people were 
working. They immediately rushed 
to the fire escapes from the five 
storey building. Some, finding the 
exits blocked, jumped from the 
windows, oa Se .

| SALINAS, Cal—The most sought-

herenm of the west’s college track- 


men including Mr. Jeffrey himself, 
Couches are unanimously agreed that 
the young Salinas speedster is 
destined for cinder immortality. 
Davis won his trial heat at the 
event in 9.8 to tie the national re- 
cord. Then he stepped out in the 
finals to humiliate his jleld in the 
rousing time of 9.5, cracking the 
junior college record, comlny with- 
in a tenth of a, secund of the 
_ world’s record and beating his 
nearest competitor by 10 yards, 
Sure, Jeffrey “game back to the

in another 9.5, but what of it? That's 
great running and - it's just what 
you'd expect from the brilliant Stan- 
ford senior.

WAR. FACTORS IN: 
FAVOUR OF BRITAIN 


LONDON, July 30 (Reuter).—The milita correspondent of 
the “Yorkshire Post” to-day summarises the factors in Britain's 


favour at the present juncture of the war. 


He says that firstly the German 

army falied in June to follow up tie 

“withdrawal of the B.E.F., when there 
wna a chance. 

Secondly, we have now -w mighty 
foree in Britain and strong defences. 

‘Thirdly, the RAP. has ‘achieved 
moral superlorlty. : 

Blockado Succcss 

Fourthly, the blockade is closing 
the last Joopholcs of supply. 

Fifthly, the enemy power has failed 
to Interrupt Imperjal ond American 
sources of supply, . 

Sixthly, there !s more food and 
mare materials jn England thon cver 
before, and the Navy sweeps ‘the 
Beas, i 

Seventhly, Hitler's forces are wide- 
ly dispersed on a sea Une from: the 
Arctic to the Pyrenees, 

Elghthly, his vast coastiine has not 
enough ships to protest Jt or ty escort 
nomaritime invasion, 

Italy A Burdon. 

Ninely, Italy's position is a burden 
on Germany, who may ‘hive to give 
material aid, especially if Maly is ate 
{nelked ug our main alfensive. 

The correspondent also polnts out 

. that Britain controls the Mediter- 
‘ranean and has separated Haly from 
her colonies. He adds that we are 
confident of repelling, massed alr 
attnckg and our fighter planes tn-

Convention 


Detailed Analysis Of 
Main Points 


HAVANA, July 30 (Reuter). 
The main points of the Con- 
vention and the “Act of Havana” 
concluded by the Pan-American 
(Conference are .now published 
here and are as follows: 

"Considering the destinies of the 
colonics of non-American countrics 
in this hemisphere, and that a situa- 
tlon may develop which mny  ex- 
tlngulsh or materially, impuir their 
sovereignty, the American Republics 
condemn all violeace. . 

“The Republics ‘consider possible 
transfers of soverelymty as against 
Americin sentiments and the rights 
of American states to maintain their 
J security and -political independence, 

Transfor Unacceptable 

“No such transfer | would be re 
copnised or accepted by the American 
Republics no‘ matter what form was 
employed. | g 

“They reserve the right to judge 
whether any transfer or attempted 
transfer has the effect of impairing 
the — political independence — of 
European possessions in this hemis- 
phere. 

“The Republics will establish a 
provisional regime for such regions 
until such time ag thelr definitive 
vovernment is establistied by free de- 
termination of the people. 

Unquestioned Right 

Tho Convention continues: 

"Republics hive the unquestioned 
right to take such regions under their 
administration and deliberate as to 
thelr destinies, though this docs not 
imply abrogation of principle of non- 
intervention, In: the case where a 
provisional rejtime is decided on, ad- 
ministration. will be. exercised by ono 
or more of the’ American States. 

“The Administration shall be in the 
interests of security of the Americas, 
and for the benefit of the region ad- 
ministered.” 

The Convention enumerates the in- 
dividual rights which are to be pre- 
served by the Administration and the 
States, 

“Open economle relations shall be 
maintained with oll countries on the 
basis of reciprocity. The first Ad- 
ministration shall be for three years, 


ten years. 

“Local. revenues 

‘Adminstration. 
Administrative Commission 

"A Commission for territorial ad- 
ninistration is to be established and 
composed of.onc representative from 
each country ratifying the Conven- 
tion. Any signatory may  convoke 
the Commission and two thirds of the 
members constitute a quorum neces- 
sary to tale. decisions. 

“None of the provisions of the 
Convention refer to territories which 
ore subject of dispute or clatins be- 
tween European powers and one or 
more of the ‘Republics of — the 
Americas. 

“The Convention shall enter into 
force when two-thirds of the Re- 
publics have ratified dt. 

"The Act of Havana repeats the 
principles of the Convention and au- 
thorises the immediate ‘creation of an 
emergency committee composed of 
one representative of each of the 
American republics, two-thirds of the 
membership being sufficient for a 
Committee to act.” 

Argentina adds the reservation that 
it regards the Falklands as part of 
Argentine territory,

WASHINGTON, July 30 (Reuter). |: 


—The Senate Military: Affairs Com- 
mittee to-day postponed final action 
on the Burke-Wadsworth Compulsory 
Military. Service Bill, the aim ‘of 
which was to. register 42,000,000.men 
between the ages of 18 and 64, 

The Committee Instead decided to 
take up a mensure authorising Pre- 
sident ‘Roosevelt to call up the 
National Guard. 


Tentative Approval 


The Senate Committee have al- |. 


ready given {entatlve approval to the 
muin features .of the Conscription 
Bill, but there are slgns of growing 
opposition to the meusure both inside 
and outside Congress. , 

One possibility, which the Come 
mittee decided, is:a reduction of the 
uge mits of those subject to active 
military tralning,’ to 21 10 31 instead 
of the present figure of 21 to 45," 


DIARY OF A. 
BRITISH NURSE 


(Continued from Page 4.) 


{hey started ngain, We've been very 
busy. When na convoy comes in 
everybody cones on duty, whatever 
hour of the day or night, and we 
work until they are. all fixed up and 
in bed. Then the staff on duty carry 
on and the rest of us go back to'bed. 
Night duty is diMcult here becnuse 
the black out is not 100 per cent., so 
we have to #o groping about the 
wards with oa, small hand torch, 

The buys are really marvellous, 
Never .complain, Always 
Always say: “Well it might have 
heen worse.” We don't mind how 
hoard we work or what we have to 
put up with, We've had a few Ger- 
mons to took after’ and they ‘were 
good patients too, : 

The most touching thing I have 
seen \yas one of our Tommies, A 
batch was getting ready for eni« 
barkatlon to Englund, All our boys 
had had a parcel from home or a 
little present from someon. A 
razor, shaving soap, cigarettes, some- 
thing. There was one German, and 
of course he had no present. So one 


bought him 


something, I'm pretty 
hardened, 


But It brought tears to 
my cyes, I couldn't help It, 

June 17, Well at lust 1 am on a 
British ship heading for Old Eng- 
land. What a four days we've had! 
Bombing, bombing all the tine. It’s 
amazing how you yet used to it. Got 
separated from F, for the first time 
since we came over. She was on 
day duty, I was on night. At 4.30 
she was put on a train for St. 
Nazaire. This Js our Just evacuation 
I keep on thinking about F, and 
wondering if she Is all right. 

L left with the last lot at 7.30 p.m. 
It's only about 10 miles to St, 
Nazaire, the port where we-were to 
get a ship, but jt was 10 o'clock when 
we arrived. About 150 of us sisters 
Were put on a tender with 500 troops. 
In the darkness the tender crept 
about trying to find a ship, Sudden- 
ly a destroyer loomed uhead, The 
officer shouted that he had 500 men: 
could they take them on board? The 
answer came “Yes.” Then he called: 
“And I've pot 150 sisters!" A bloni 
sftence followed ‘that announcement. 
Then after a few moments: “You 
had better follow me. We've got no 
accommodation for women.” 

So the tender followed the de- 
stroyer for a while. until we came 
alongside a Mner and we were all 
taken aboard. Still we weren't out 
of the wood. They were bombing 
every ship they could find, but we 
didn't get hit. It toole us two days 
to cross. And were we glad to see 
England! 

June 19. 
I got to London was to go to head-. 
quarters, I'found out thero that F. 
Was safe und ‘I was never so glad to 
see anyone In my life. She told me 
that, two bombs had dropped one at 
each end of the ship they were on, 
while It was waiting in the harbour, 
but nobody got a scratch, ‘ The 
‘planes pursttcd them, toa, after they 
were ot sea but nobody was hurt. 
She's lucky to be alive. Her chiet 
worry Is whether sale will ever ‘see 
her trunte again os she has only the 
clothes she stands up in. We've both 
still got ‘our tin. hats, though, and 
wo've got quite attached to them. 
We fcel-a lot safer, with them. on. 
“We go on ten days’. leave ‘now. 
When Wwe report for duty I 
where we shall be . sent. I don't 
mind where it is, because the Army 
looks after us so well, and I wouldn't 
havo missed the experience of dolng 
active service for anything.

Desert 
Gallantry 


Decorations For Officers 
Of Middle East 


2 London, July 30. 
Gallantry in desert fighting Is re- 
copnised by the immediate award by 
General Wavell, Commanding the 
Middle East, of a bar to the Military 


.| Cross, three Military Crosses and two 


Milltary Medals to‘officers and _non- 
commissioned officers of the Royal 


| Armoured Corps (Hussars). 


Licut. Delme Seymour-Evans, MC. 
receives a bar for bold leadership of 
n erulser squadron which was large- 
ly responsible for the capture of a 
fort. In a later action he advanced 
under enemy fire straight at the 
guns, with the. result that 12 enemy 
tanks and all four puns were destroy- 
ed and the infantry laid down their 
arms. 

Other recipients are Crosses to 
2nd/Lieut. Corrie Halliday, 2nd/ 
Liout. Robin Oates and 2nd, Licut. 
Warren Gnpe, and Medals to 
Sergeant Thomas Bowyer and 
Corporal Arthur James Taber.— 
Reuter.
